  CS6650 Assignment 1: SkiTrak — Swipe Ingest Server and Multithreaded Load     
  Client                                                                        
                                                                                
  Overview                                                                      
                                                                                
  You work for SkiTrak, a company that builds real-time infrastructure for ski  
  resorts. Every time a skier boards a lift, a swipe event is generated.        
  SkiTrak ingests these events at scale, tracks each skier's stats, and drives  
  a live leaderboard of the top skiers by vertical feet at each resort.         
  Across this series of assignments you will build a scalable, distributed,     
  cloud-hosted version of this system:                                          
  * Assignment 1 — Build a lightweight swipe-ingest server and a multithreaded  
  clien                                                                         
  ──── (255 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  l multithreaded WebSocket client you can build on.                            
  Client Connection Management Tips                                             
                                                                                
  1. Separate the two paths — an HTTP connection pool for ingest, and a         
  dedicated set of persistent WebSocket connections for subscriptions.          
  2. Reconnect on drop — a WebSocket can close mid-run; detect it and           
  re-subscribe.                                                                 
  3. Don't let the generator block senders — hand swipes to the sender pool     
  through a thread-safe queue.                                                  
  Performance Testing Tools                                                     
                                                                                
  * VisualVM — profile your Java client                                         
  * wscat / Postman — manual WebSocket testing                                  
  * Chrome DevTools — WebSocket frame inspector                                 
  AWS EC2 Setup                                                                 
                                                                                
  Follow Lab 1. In short:                                                       
  1. Launch a t2.micro (free tier) in us-west-2                                 
  2. Security group inbound: port 8080 (Tomcat) and port 22 (SSH), from your IP 
  3. Install Java 11 and Tomcat 9 (as in Lab 1)                                 
  4. Deploy your WAR to webapps/ (as in Lab 2, Part 4)                          
  5. Use screen or systemd to keep Tomcat running                               
  Common Pitfalls to Avoid                                                      
                                                                                
  * Treating the server as the thing to optimize — it's a dummy; optimize       
  the client                                                                    
  * Blocking the ingest sender threads on the message-generation thread         
  * Not pooling / reusing HTTP connections on the ingest path                   
  * Forgetting to remove WebSocket sessions on close, leaking the subscriber    
  set                                                                           
  * Not handling WebSocket close/reconnect on the client                        
  * Comparing measured throughput to Little's Law without explaining the gap    
  AI Usage Reflection (up to +5 points)                                         
                                                                                
  This course expects you to use AI coding assistants. Submit it                
  as AI_USAGE.md in your repo (or append it to your design document).           
  Include:                                                                      
  * Where and for what you used AI tools — be specific about the parts (e.g.,   
  scaffolding the sender thread pool, debugging a connection leak, writing the  
  latency-CSV code, generating swipe test data, drafting the WebSocket          
  subscriber).                                                                  
  * At least two suggestions you accepted and two you rejected or had to        
  correct — and why. This is the part that shows understanding.                 
  * One thing the AI got wrong, or a decision you had to reason through         
  yourself rather than take from the AI (e.g., the Little's Law prediction,     
  thread-pool sizing, warmup-vs-steady-state measurement, WebSocket connection  
  management).                                                                  
  * Recommended: share your chat transcript(s). Include a share link from your  
  AI tool, or export the conversation into a transcripts/ folder in your repo.  
  Sharing the actual exchange is the easiest and most convincing way to show    
  where you exercised judgment, and makes your reflection concrete rather than  
  a summary. How it's graded: points reward honesty and insight, not the amount 
  of AI used.

  3. Bugs, concurrency issues and wrong assumptions

  B1 (server, high): one exception permanently stops the leaderboard timer. In
  LeaderboardEndpoint.pushAll(), nothing catches exceptions.
  ScheduledExecutorService.scheduleAtFixedRate silently cancels all future runs
  if a task throws. Tomcat's getAsyncRemote().sendText() throws
  IllegalStateException if an earlier async send to that session is still in
  flight (TEXT_FULL_WRITING), for example with a slow subscriber or many
  subscribers. A session closing between isOpen() and sendText() has the same
  effect. One bad session stops pushes for every resort until Tomcat restarts.
  Fix: wrap each per-session send, and the whole loop body, in try/catch. This
  affects the 3-point WebSocket rubric item.

  B2 (server, medium): the pusher thread leaks on redeploy. The static
  single-thread executor uses a non-daemon thread and is never shut down.
  Redeploying the WAR in Tomcat leaks the thread and the old classloader, and
  Tomcat logs a warning. Fix: shut it down from a
  ServletContextListener.contextDestroyed. This counts toward "clean code".

  B3 (server, low): @PathParam resortID isn't validated, so /leaderboard/abc
  gets subscribed. onError removes the session but doesn't close it. Tomcat
  usually calls onClose afterwards, so this is minor.

  B4 (client, measurement): ingest latency includes retry backoff sleeps (see
  Part 3).

  B5 (client, verification gap): the "never block senders" claim rests on
  queue.take() being fast in practice. There's no instrumentation or head start
  (see Part 2).

  B6 (client, metric gap): no HTTP connection statistics.

  B7 (client): failedSwipes is collected but never output.

  Concurrency checks that passed:
  - The per-worker counters and records lists aren't thread-safe, but each one
    belongs to a single worker. The main thread reads them only after
    Future.get(), which guarantees it sees the final values. Correct.
  - remainingSwipes.getAndDecrement() > 0 hands out exactly N take() calls,
    matching the producer's N put() calls. There's no deadlock or leftover work,
    and the logs confirm 500,000/500,000.
  - In LeaderboardSubscriber, the shared state is guarded by synchronized(this)
    and webSocket is volatile. Each Listener has its own AtomicBoolean guard, so
    one connection can't trigger two reconnects. If a connect completes after
    stop(), the !running check aborts it. The default onOpen calls request(1),
    and onText keeps requesting.
  - A stalled subscriber's 10 s awaitConnected wait delays the start but doesn't
    break anything.

  Assumptions to state in the report:
  - The resulting throughput is limited by latency, not by the server:
    throughput ≈ threads / latency at 64, 128, 192 and 256 threads. The client
    is therefore close to theoretical for each thread count. To go faster you
    need more concurrent requests in flight (more threads, or async sendAsync),
    and you need to find where the t2.micro starts to saturate. You stopped at
    256 threads, so you haven't found that ceiling yet.
  - Warmup takes about 23 s, roughly a third of the 71 s total at w256, and
    can't be shortened under the spec. That's why overall throughput (7,030/s)
    is much lower than main-phase throughput (9,779/s). Report both.
  - Localhost runs get slower with more threads (39k/s at 64 → 25k/s at 256)
    because client and server share the CPU. Keep them as supporting data only.
    Grading should be based on the EC2 runs.

⏺ Here's the plan. Nothing has been changed yet. Two of your rules conflict with
  what the code actually does, so I've listed those first.

  Two conflicts with the current code

  1. The server can't drop Gson without a code change. SkierServlet parses the
  request body and writes error messages with Gson. LeaderboardEndpoint builds
  its leaderboard JSON with Gson. If the server pom has only the servlet and
  websocket APIs, the server won't compile unless I rewrite that JSON code,
  which would break your "no business logic changes" rule.

  I recommend keeping gson as a normal (compile-scope) dependency in the server.
  It's a server library, not a client one, so the "no Tyrus/HttpClient in
  WEB-INF/lib" check still passes. Your current WAR already ships it.

  2. The clients have no third-party dependencies, and they share no classes 
  with the server. The client uses only the JDK:
  - HTTP: java.net.http.HttpClient
  - WebSocket: java.net.http.WebSocket
  - JSON: built by hand with string concatenation in Swipe.toJsonBody(), and
    serverTimestamp is read with indexOf

  It never touches LiftRide, ResponseMsg or Leaderboard, since it has its own
  client.modal.Swipe. So there's nothing to duplicate, and the client poms will
  declare no dependencies. Adding unused Tyrus, Apache HttpClient or Gson would
  only bloat the jars. The shade plugin still builds an executable jar with the
  main class set.

  Where each file goes

  Package names stay the same (server.*, client.*), so no package or import
  lines need to change.

⏺ Confirmed: your local Tomcat sends Connection: close on the 100th and 200th
  response and then drops the connection. The JDK HttpClient exposes that header
  and silently opens a new connection. A 500,000-swipe run therefore replaces
  connections roughly 5,000 times, and the client currently reports none of it.

  Everything you list for removal is already gone (SwipeRecord, ThroughputChart,
  per-swipe timing, CSV, PNG chart, per-resort throughput). Nothing references
  them, and no further removal is needed for those.

  How I'd add #5 with as little change as possible

  The ingest path uses the JDK's java.net.http.HttpClient, which has no API for
  pool or connection statistics. Switching to Apache HttpClient would make exact
  counts possible, but it would replace the request code you asked me to keep.
  So I'll stay on the JDK client and count connection events from what each
  response already tells us:

  - Reconnections are counted directly. A reconnection is any event that forces
    the next request onto a new TCP connection:
    - Server-closed: the response carries Connection: close (Tomcat's
      100-requests-per-connection limit, which the probe confirmed).
    - Connection errors: client.send throws IOException, i.e. the connection
      broke and the retry opens a new one.
  - Connections opened is derived, not counted directly: peak concurrent senders
    (max(32, mainWorkers)) plus reconnections. Each worker sends synchronously,
    so it holds at most one connection at a time, and the JDK pool reuses idle
    ones. The output will say it's derived. The JDK gives no way to count
    connection opens directly short of internal-API hacks, which I'd rule out.

  Files I'll change (only these two)

  client-part1/src/main/java/client/worker/SwipeWorker.java
  - Add per-worker counters serverClosedCount and connectionErrorCount with
    getters, following the same pattern as retryCount. They're read only after
    Future.get(), so no synchronization is needed.
  - In send(), keep the response in a local variable so its connection header
    can be checked. The catch (IOException) block increments
    connectionErrorCount.
  - The request, timeout, status handling, retry loop and backoff stay exactly
    as they are.

  client-part1/src/main/java/client/LoadTestClient.java
  - Add an "HTTP Connection Statistics" section to the results, overall and per
    phase: server-closed reconnections, connection-error reconnections, total
    reconnections, and derived connections opened, with a one-line note
    explaining the derivation.

  Unchanged: server/, client-part2/, LeaderboardSubscriber, SwipeProducer,
  SwipeGenerator, Swipe, RttMeasurement, the tests and the pom.

  Verification afterwards:
  - mvn clean package, then run both test programs.
  - One full 500,000-swipe run against your local Tomcat, which should take
    about 15 s based on your earlier localhost runs. This checks the new numbers
    make sense: about 5,000 server-closed reconnections, consistent with
    Connection: close every 100 responses.

  The client also prints some numbers that aren't on your list. Should they
  stay?

⏺ client-part1 now reports all eight Part 1 metrics, including the HTTP
  connection statistics that were missing. It builds and both tests pass. I
  changed only SwipeWorker.java and LoadTestClient.java; server/, client-part2/
  and everything else are untouched.

  What changed:
  - client/worker/SwipeWorker.java:
    - Two new per-worker counters, serverClosedCount and connectionErrorCount,
      with getters.
    - send() now keeps the response in a variable so it can check the
      Connection: close header, and the catch (IOException) block counts the
      error.
    - The request, timeout, status handling, retry loop and backoff are
      unchanged.
  - client/LoadTestClient.java: a new "HTTP Connection Statistics (ingest)"
    section in the output, broken down by warmup, main and total. Nothing else
    changed.

  Everything else you listed for removal (per-swipe timing, SwipeRecord,
  ThroughputChart, CSV, PNG chart, per-resort throughput) was already gone from
  the previous step. As you chose, the extra output (retries, per-phase
  breakdown, WebSocket drops and reconnects) stays.

  Full run: 500,000 swipes, 128 main workers, against your local Tomcat
  - Ingest: 500,000 successful, 0 failed. 15.93 s total, of which warmup took
    1.44 s. Overall throughput 31,391 swipes/s.
  - HTTP connections: 4,939 server-closed reconnections plus 1 connection error,
    so 4,940 reconnections in total and about 5,068 connections opened
    (derived).
  - Subscribers: all 10 connected, received 32 updates each, and took 35–44 ms
    to connect.
  - Push latency: mean 0.3 ms, median 0, p95 1 ms, p99 2 ms. It's near zero
    because client and server share a clock on localhost.

  How to read the connection numbers:
  - Reconnections are counted directly. Tomcat closes each keep-alive connection
    after 100 requests, which a probe confirmed on the 100th and 200th
    response. That predicts about 320 reconnections in warmup and 4,680 in main.
    The run measured 316 and 4,623; the small shortfall is connections that
    were still under 100 requests when the phase ended.
  - The one connection error was retried and succeeded. It's most likely a
    request that raced with Tomcat closing the connection.
  - Connections opened is derived, not counted: peak concurrent senders plus
    reconnections, because the JDK HttpClient exposes no pool statistics. The
    output says so.

  Worth putting in your report: about 1 in 100 requests pays for a new TCP
  connection because of Tomcat's maxKeepAliveRequests=100 default. The effect is
  much bigger on EC2, where a connection setup costs a full network round trip.
  It's part of why measured throughput falls short of the Little's Law
  prediction.

  The full output is saved in my scratchpad at runcheck/p1_full_run.log. The run
  wrote no files into the project.

❯ It seems like currently we don't have connections and reconnections status in 
  the ingest path, which refers to SwipeWorker                                  

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You're right that SwipeWorker doesn't track connections or reconnections
  itself. It only records the two raw signals, serverClosedCount (responses with
  Connection: close) and connectionErrorCount (IOExceptions). The actual
  "connections" and "reconnections" numbers are calculated afterwards in
  LoadTestClient.printConnectionStats (lines 151–177):
  - reconnections = closed + errors
  - connections opened = worker count + reconnections

  So the ingest statistics are printed, but the code that defines them lives
  outside the worker that sends the requests.

  That calculation also slightly overcounts reconnections. A Connection: close
  on a worker's final request is counted as a reconnection even though no new
  connection follows.

  Proposed change (needs your approval): have SwipeWorker count connections and
  reconnections itself, at the point where it knows its next request needs a new
  connection.

  - SwipeWorker.java:
    - Add connectionCount and reconnectionCount with getters, and a private
      needsNewConnection flag that starts as true.
    - Before each client.send: if the flag is set, increment connectionCount. If
      this isn't the worker's first connection, also increment
      reconnectionCount. Then clear the flag.
    - Set the flag again after a Connection: close response or an IOException,
      where the existing counters are incremented now.
    - Keep serverClosedCount and connectionErrorCount as the breakdown of why
      reconnections happened.
    - This only adds counting: the request, retries and backoff don't change,
      and your comments and formatting stay.
  - LoadTestClient.java: printConnectionStats simply adds up
    getConnectionCount() and getReconnectionCount() per phase. The calculation
    in the printer goes away, and the table keeps the ServerClosed and
    ConnErrors columns.

  Caveat that stays either way: the JDK HttpClient shares one connection pool
  across all workers. A worker's "new connection" may actually be an idle pooled
  connection left by another worker, for example a main-phase worker reusing a
  warmup connection. The per-worker counts are therefore the connections each
  worker needed, not proven TCP opens. The output will keep a short note saying
  so.

  After the change I'd rebuild, run both tests, and do one full run against your
  local Tomcat.

  Should I go ahead?

  The current numbers break down as follows:
  - Measured exactly: serverClosedCount (responses with Connection: close) and
    connectionErrorCount (IOExceptions), both counted in SwipeWorker.

    "ConnectionsOpened" = workers + reconnections.

  connections can't be counted. The table's exact-looking ConnectionsOpened
  column overstates what's known. It will become estimates marked with ~ and
  labelled "estimate", printed next to the two measured counts.

⏺ The output now shows exactly the eight metrics, in three sections: Ingest,
  Connection Statistics and Subscribe. Only LoadTestClient.java changed. It
  builds, both tests pass, and a full 500,000-swipe run against your local
  Tomcat completed: 500,000 successful, 0 failed, 13.7 s total, 36,428 swipes/s.

  What changed in LoadTestClient.java:
  - Run header: Target and Main workers are now one configuration line. The
    phase "finished" lines are gone; "starting" stays as progress.
  - Ingest section: replaces the two per-phase blocks and "Overall Results". It
    shows successful, failed (after 5 retries), warmup, main and total runtime,
    and overall throughput. printPhase is deleted.
  - Workload check: the "matches workload" line now appears only as a WARNING
    when the counts don't add up.
  - Connection statistics: server-closed responses and connection errors are
    printed as measured counts. Reconnections (~4,940) and connections opened
    (~5,068) carry ~ and the label "estimate". A note says actual TCP
    connections aren't counted. The per-phase table and the exact-looking
    ConnectionsOpened* column are gone.
  - Subscribe section:
    - The table shows Subscriber, Resort, Updates and FirstConnect(ms).
    - The WebSocket connect-time line now includes the reconnect count.
    - Push latency is one line with mean, median, p95, p99 and the clock-skew
      note.
    - The total-updates line is gone.
    - If no subscriber connects or no updates arrive, the connect-time and
      push-latency lines print n/a instead of being left out.

  Unchanged:
  - Workload, threads and phases: thread counts, the queue, swipe generation,
    and warmup and main behaviour.
  - HTTP behaviour: the requests, retry limit and backoff, and connection reuse.
  - Tracking: the failed-swipe list and WebSocket reconnect handling.
  - Counters: all of them in SwipeWorker and LeaderboardSubscriber, including
    retries, drops and failed swipes, even where they're no longer printed.

  Limits of the connection numbers (also noted in the code comment):
  - Only server-closed responses and IOExceptions are actually measured.
  - Reconnections can overcount by up to one per worker. A Connection: close on
    a worker's last request is counted even though no new connection follows.
  - Reconnections miss connections the server closes silently, for example on
    idle timeout without an error.
  - Connections opened is the worker count plus that estimate, not an observed
    number.

  Getting exact counts would mean replacing the JDK HttpClient, which this task
  ruled out.

  The run log is in my scratchpad at runcheck/p1_simplified_run.log. Nothing was
  written into the project.

⏺ Yes: client-part2 needs per-swipe records in SwipeWorker. Specifically a list
  of SwipeRecords, one per swipe, not a single SwipeRecord property. Right now
  client-part2 doesn't compile.

  What's wrong now:
  - client-part2/.../worker/SwipeWorker.java is the stripped-down Part 1
    version. It has the new connection counters but no records list and no
    getRecords().
  - client-part2/.../LoadTestClient.java still has the full Part 3 code, and
    lines 158–159 call w.getRecords(). I compiled the sources into my scratchpad
    and got two cannot find symbol errors at exactly those lines.
  - SwipeRecord.java and ThroughputChart.java are still in client-part2. Nothing
    creates SwipeRecord objects any more.

  Why Part 2 needs it. The spec's Part 3 ("client with full performance
  analysis") asks for, per swipe:
  - timestamp before send
  - timestamp when the response is received
  - latency in ms
  - a CSV row {timestamp, requestType, latency, statusCode, resortID}

  From those it computes the mean, median, p95, p99, min and max, the throughput
  per resort, and the 10-second throughput chart. SwipeRecord holds exactly
  those fields, and LoadTestClient reads them from the list. Without the
  records, none of that Part 3 output can be produced.

  What to add to client-part2's SwipeWorker. The previous version had exactly
  this. Merged with your current file, so the connection counters stay, it looks
  like:

  import client.modal.SwipeRecord;
  ...
      // Stores swipes that fail after all allowed retries.
      private final List<Swipe> failedSwipes = new ArrayList<>();

      // One record per swipe for the Part 3 latency analysis.
      private final List<SwipeRecord> records = new ArrayList<>();
  ...
                  Swipe swipe = queue.take();

                  // Time the swipe from just before sending until the final 
  response.
                  long sendTimeMillis = System.currentTimeMillis();
                  long startNanos = System.nanoTime();
                  int status = send(swipe);
                  double latencyMs = (System.nanoTime() - startNanos) / 1e6;
                  long responseTimeMillis = System.currentTimeMillis();

                  SwipeRecord record = new SwipeRecord(sendTimeMillis,
  responseTimeMillis,
                          latencyMs, status, swipe.getResortID());
                  records.add(record);

                  if (record.isSuccess()) {
                      successCount++;
                  } else {
                      failureCount++;
                      failedSwipes.add(swipe);
                  }
  ...
      // Return this worker's per-swipe records.
      public List<SwipeRecord> getRecords() {
          return records;
      }

  Design points for the report:
  - Thread safety: each worker owns its own records list, and LoadTestClient
    reads it only after Future.get(), which guarantees it sees the final
    contents. A plain ArrayList is correct, and a synchronized or concurrent
    list would only add contention to the hot path.
  - Timer choice: nanoTime is used for the latency because it's monotonic.
    currentTimeMillis is used for the CSV timestamp and the chart buckets
    because those need wall-clock time.
  - Memory: 500,000 small records come to roughly 20–30 MB, which is fine.
  - Retries are inside the timed window, because the timing wraps the whole
    send(). A retried swipe's latency therefore includes 100–1,600 ms of backoff
    sleep. With 0–1 retries per run this barely moves the results, but say so
    in the report. The alternative is to time only the final attempt inside
    send(), which reflects a single request's round trip more precisely.

  Optional: LoadTestClient in client-part2 doesn't print the HTTP connection
  statistics that client-part1 now prints, even though SwipeWorker already
  counts them. You could copy printConnectionStats over.

  Earlier you said you'd add the Part 2 analysis yourself, so I haven't changed
  anything. I can apply the SwipeWorker change, with or without timing only the
  final attempt, and confirm client-part2 builds again.

  What I added to SwipeWorker:
  - The list: private final List<SwipeRecord> records and getRecords(), plus the
    SwipeRecord import.
  - Timing: in run(), a wall-clock send time and a nanoTime start are captured
    just before send(swipe). Latency and response time are taken right after it
    returns.
  - One record per swipe: exactly one record is created whether the swipe
    succeeds or fails, with the final status, or 0 if no response arrived. That
    keeps the CSV row count equal to the number of swipes.
  - Retries: latency covers all attempts including backoff. That's what
    SwipeRecord's Javadoc and the "time includes any retries" output already
    describe.
  - Success test: success and failure now come from record.isSuccess(), which is
    the same 2xx rule as before.
  - Unchanged: failedSwipes, the retry count, both connection counters, and the
    request and retry code.
  - Thread safety: only the worker's own thread writes to its list.
    LoadTestClient reads it only after Future.get(), which guarantees it sees
    the final contents, so a plain ArrayList is correct. A comment in the code
    says so.

  LeaderboardSubscriber and the WebSocket and push-latency logic weren't
  touched.

⏺ The per-message push latency is captured correctly, but it's held only in
  memory and never written to a file. That's enough for the assignment:
  per-message records are only required for swipes, not leaderboard messages. I
  didn't change any code.

  First, something unrelated that needs your attention: your earlier test 
  results are gone from results/. The folder now holds only
  per_swipe_metrics.csv and throughput_over_time.png, both written at 20:24.
  These are no longer there:

  - the localhost runs LOCALHOST_w64 through LOCALHOST_w256
  - LOCALHOST_rtt_measurement.log

  results/ itself was last modified at 20:27. None of my commands touch that
  folder, and the files aren't in the macOS Trash. If you moved them somewhere,
  that's fine. If not, they're your only EC2 evidence, so check Time Machine or
  any copy you have.

  The findings below are for client-part2. Its LeaderboardSubscriber.java is
  identical to the one in client-part1.

  1. Is receiveTime captured on receipt, and is latency receiveTime − 
  serverTimestamp?

  Yes to both.

  The receive time is captured at the top of the listener callback
  (client-part2/src/main/java/client/websocket/LeaderboardSubscriber.java):
  233            long receiveMillis = System.currentTimeMillis();
  234            partial.append(data);
  236            if (last) {
  238                recordMessage(partial.toString(), receiveMillis);
  - It's captured the moment the JDK delivers the text frame, before any
    parsing.
  - A message split into several fragments uses the time of its final fragment.
    The server's leaderboard JSON is small, so in practice each message arrives
    in one frame.

  serverTimestamp is pulled out of the JSON and converted to epoch milliseconds
  (lines 191–213):
  202            long serverMillis = Instant.parse(
  203                    json.substring(startIdx, endIdx)
  204            ).toEpochMilli();

  The subtraction itself happens later, when the results are printed (lines
  323–331):
  326        for (long[] message : messages) {
  327            latencies.add(message[0] - message[1]);   // receiveMillis - 
  serverMillis
  Calculating it later gives the same value as calculating it on arrival,
  because both timestamps are fixed when the message is recorded.

  Caveats:
  - Both timestamps have millisecond resolution, which is why the localhost runs
    show 0–3 ms.
  - The two values come from different machines' clocks, so the result includes
    clock skew. That's why your EC2 runs showed negative latencies.
  - A message with a missing or unparseable serverTimestamp is silently skipped
    (lines 193–195 and 210–212), so it's counted neither as an update nor in the
    latency.

  2. Where is each individual value stored?

  The raw timestamp pair is stored once per message, and the latency is computed
  from it on request.

  What: Storage field
  Where: LeaderboardSubscriber.java:51: private final List<long[]> messages = 
  new
   ArrayList<>();
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: One pair added per message, under a lock
  Where: LeaderboardSubscriber.java:206–209: synchronized (this) { 
  messages.add(new long[]{receiveMillis, serverMillis}); }
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: Per-message latencies built as a new list
  Where: LeaderboardSubscriber.java:323–331: getPushLatenciesMs()
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: Collected across all subscribers
  Where: client-part2/src/main/java/client/LoadTestClient.java:282 and :290:
  allLatencies.addAll(s.getPushLatenciesMs());
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: Sorted and summarized
  Where: LoadTestClient.java:322–339: mean, percentile(allLatencies, 50/95/99)

  Each subscriber guards its list with synchronized, and the getter returns a
  copy, so there's no data race between the WebSocket callback threads and the
  main thread.

  3. Memory only, or also written to a file?

  Memory only. The only file client-part2 writes is the per-swipe CSV, built
  from SwipeRecord (LoadTestClient.java:181–182, writeSwipeCsv(records, 
  RESULTS_DIR.resolve(SWIPE_CSV)), with SWIPE_CSV = "per_swipe_metrics.csv" at
  line 54).

  Nothing writes messages or the latency list anywhere. Once the summary is
  printed and the program exits, the individual push latencies are gone.

  4. Does the assignment require per-message records to be saved?

  No. The repository has no README or copy of the assignment, so I checked
  against the specification you pasted at the start of this session. The
  relevant lines:
  - Subscribe path (Part 2): "For each synthetic leaderboard message received,
    record the receipt time and the message's serverTimestamp."
  - Push-latency metrics (Part 3): "For each leaderboard message received,
    compute receiveTime − serverTimestamp. This is an approximate one-way push
    latency," plus the clock-skew note, which must be stated in the report.
  - Statistical analysis: "Push latency: mean, median, p95, p99; total
    leaderboard updates delivered across all subscribers."
  - The only file-output requirement is for swipes: "Write to CSV: {timestamp,
    requestType, latency, statusCode, resortID} (here requestType is always
    SWIPE)."
  - The submission screenshots ask for "ingest + push-latency statistics", i.e.
    the summary.

  So for push latency, the spec asks you to record per message, compute per
  message, and report summary statistics. It never asks for a per-message file.

  5. Does the current code meet the requirement?

  Yes:
  - "Record the receipt time and serverTimestamp": done in memory by
    messages.add(new long[]{receiveMillis, serverMillis}). "Record" doesn't say
    "save to a file".
  - "Compute receiveTime − serverTimestamp" for every message: done by
    getPushLatenciesMs(), one value per stored message.
  - Mean, median, p95, p99 and total updates: all printed by client-part2.
  - Clock-skew caveat: printed in the console. You still need to state it in
    your report.

  A per-message CSV would be an optional extra, for example columns subscriber,
  resortID, serverTimestamp, receiveTime, latencyMs. It would make the negative
  EC2 latencies easier to explain, since you could show a constant offset over
  time. It isn't required, though, and leaving it out costs no points.

  LoadTestClient no longer prints any subscribe results. printSubscribeResults

  subscribers still connect, receive messages and record data, but these Part 1
  metrics never appear:
  - updates received per subscriber
  - WebSocket connection time
  - push latency

  The client-part1 README has a Known gap section saying so, and points to
  client-part2 until the output is restored. If the removal wasn't deliberate,
  it's worth fixing before submission.

  client-part1/README.md:
  - Purpose, a class table, the threading model (producer, queue, warmup and
    main phases), and error handling: 5 retries with 100–1,600 ms backoff, a 10
    s request timeout, and WebSocket reconnect from 100 ms up to 5 s.
  - Build, and run commands with the arguments and defaults.
  - RttMeasurement for the Little's Law baseline.
  - The current output: the progress lines, the Ingest section, and the
    Connection Statistics section. A table separates the two measured counts
    from the two estimates, with the estimates' limits explained.
  - EC2 results marked [TO BE COMPLETED AFTER EC2 TESTING].

  client-part2/README.md:
  - What it adds over Part 1: SwipeRecord, records in SwipeWorker,
    ThroughputChart.
  - How it measures:
    - Ingest latency wraps each whole swipe, retries included, with exactly one
      record per swipe, including failures.
    - Push latency includes clock skew, and the individual values stay in memory
      only.
    - Percentiles use the nearest-rank method.
  - Build, and running it from the repository root so files land in results/.
  - Its full console output, in order:
    - per-phase results
    - Ingest
    - ingest latency
    - per-resort throughput
    - subscribe results with push-latency percentiles
  - It doesn't print HTTP connection statistics.
  - The CSV format and chart description. Each run overwrites both files.
  - How to run the two test programs.
  - EC2 placeholders, plus a note that the files in results/ now come from a
    localhost run.

  The root README.md is now out of date in two places:
  - It says Client Part 1 prints a Subscribe section. It doesn't any more.
  - It describes Part 2's console output as "per-phase results … and overall
    results". It actually prints the per-phase blocks followed by the new === 
    Ingest === block.

      167 +    private static void printSubscribeResults(List<Leaderboard
          +Subscriber> subscribers) {                                    
      168 +        System.out.println();

      174 +

          +es.get(0)));

          +connects.

      193 +            System.out.println("WebSocket connect time: n/a (n
          +o subscriber connected)");                                    
      194 +        } else {                                              
      195 +            double sum = 0;                                   
      196 +            for (double t : allConnectTimes) {                
      197 +                sum += t;                                     
      198 +            }                                                 
      199 +                                                              
      200 +            System.out.printf(                                
      201 +                    "WebSocket connect time: mean %.1f ms, min
          + %.1f ms, max %.1f ms  (%d connections, %d reconnects)%n",    
      202 +                    sum / allConnectTimes.size(),             
      203 +                    Collections.min(allConnectTimes),         
      204 +                    Collections.max(allConnectTimes),         
      205 +                    allConnectTimes.size(),                   
      206 +                    totalReconnects);                         
      207 +        }

  The FirstConnect column is each subscriber's first handshake. The summary line
  covers every successful connection, so reconnects are included when any
  occur. If no subscriber connects, it prints WebSocket connect time: n/a
  instead of failing.

  Are the numbers real or estimated? Both are real.
  - Update counts: getUpdateCount() returns the number of entries recordMessage
    stored, one for each leaderboard message that arrived through onText with a
    parseable serverTimestamp. It's a count, not an estimate. As a cross-check,
    the server pushes every 500 ms and the subscribers were connected for
    roughly 15.8 s plus startup and shutdown, which predicts about 31–32
    messages each. Each received 31.
  - Connection times: getConnectTimesMs() records System.nanoTime() from the
    start of buildAsync to the completion of each successful handshake. The
    values differ per subscriber: 42.0 ms for the first, which pays one-time
    setup costs, then 33–35 ms. Reconnects come from the same list (its size
    minus 1), so they're counted too.

  Sections:
  1. Architecture: the client JVM (producer → queue → worker pool → shared
     HttpClient, plus 10 subscribers) and the Tomcat WAR (SkierServlet,
     HealthServlet, LeaderboardEndpoint). Blue arrows are the POST and its
     201/4xx/5xx response; orange arrows are the WebSocket handshake and the 500
     ms server push.

     which exist only in part1 or part2.

     - warmup of 32 workers × 1,000 swipes, then the main phase sharing one
       counter
     - blocking sends over the shared client
     - retry and failure counting, and how results are collected without locks
     - a paragraph on how Part 1 and Part 2 differ
  4. WebSocket connection management: how the 10 subscribers connect and receive
     messages, the drop handling and backoff that the code really implements,
     and the limitations.
  5. Little's Law: a calculation table plus notes on measurement windows,
     interpretation and predictions:
     - L = 11,760.5 × 0.03327 ≈ 391, against 400 workers.
     - The latency implied by 400 workers is 400 ÷ 11,760.5 ≈ 34.0 ms, 2.2%
       above the 33.27 ms you measured.

  For your review:
  - Measurement windows don't match. Throughput covers the main phase only, but
    the 33.27 ms mean also includes the 32,000 warmup swipes, and you can't get
    a main-only mean from the numbers you gave. The latency is also measured by
    the client, not the server, since the server records no timing. The document
    says all this rather than adjusting the figures.
  - The predictions are estimates, not measurements. They are N ÷ 34 ms: about
    940/s at 32 workers, 3,760/s at 128 and 7,530/s at 256. I stated them as
    lower bounds for fewer than 400 workers, on the assumption that latency
    drops when fewer requests are in flight.
  - There is no RTT baseline yet. The doc notes that a RttMeasurement run on EC2
    hasn't been recorded and is needed.
  - I assumed your figures are from one EC2 run of the current code. Nothing in
    the repo confirms this: results/per_swipe_metrics.csv is an older localhost
    run, so I didn't use it.
  - The default workload in the doc is 532,000 swipes (32,000 warmup + 500,000
    main), because that is what TOTAL_SWIPES currently says in the code. The
    READMEs still say 500,000.
  - Limitations the doc lists as known:
    - server: pushAll() has no exception handling, and its executor is never
      shut down
    - client: there is no heartbeat, and messages lost during a disconnect can't
      be detected
  - Package rename: the producer and generator now live in client.generator
    (they used to be in client.sender). The doc uses class names only, so it's
    unaffected.

❯ do you change the code for statistics in client-part1 and client-part2?       

⏺ No, not in the last two changes. I didn't change how either client collects
  statistics; I only changed how LoadTestClient prints them.

  What I changed:
  - client-part1 LoadTestClient:
    - added a printPhase() method that prints the Warmup and Main sections
    - renamed the === Ingest === block to === Overall Results ===
    - moved the per-phase runtime lines into the phase sections
  - client-part2 LoadTestClient:
    - renamed the === Ingest === block to === Overall Results ===
    - removed the duplicate "Warmup runtime" / "Main runtime" lines
    - changed the wording and units of its existing printPhase() to match Part 1

  What I didn't change, in either client:
  - SwipeWorker's counters for successes, failures, retries, Connection: close
    responses and connection errors, and Part 2's per-swipe records
  - how phases are timed (runWorkers()) and how total runtime is measured
  - how counters are summed (sumSuccess, sumFailure)
  - LeaderboardSubscriber and the latency, per-resort and subscribe calculations

  The phase sections only read numbers that were already being collected. The
  retry count, for example, comes from the existing SwipeWorker.getRetryCount(),

⏺ Yes. Both clients already print a per-resort count of leaderboard messages
  received, not just connection results, so no code change is needed. The only
  gap is that the console shows counts, not the push content itself.

  1. How messages are counted (LeaderboardSubscriber)

  The class is identical in both clients:
  - connect() releases firstConnect.countDown() only after a handshake succeeds,
    and records that handshake's duration in connectTimesMs.
  - onText → recordMessage() adds an entry to messages (line 208) only when a
    complete message arrives containing a parseable serverTimestamp. Only the
    server's synthetic leaderboard JSON contains that field.
  - getUpdateCount() returns messages.size(), so a non-zero count means real
    pushes from the server were received and parsed.

  2. Which output shows what

  Shows: Connection succeeded
  client-part1: Subscribers connected: n/10 (printed before ingest starts)
  client-part2: same line
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Shows:
  client-part1: FirstConnect(ms) per subscriber, and the WebSocket connect time 
  …
   (N connections, R reconnects) line
  client-part2: Connect(ms), Drops and Reconnects per subscriber, and the
  Connection establishment time line
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Shows: Messages received
  client-part1: Updates column in === Subscribe ===, one row per resort 1–10
  client-part2: Updates column in === Subscribe Results ===
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Shows:
  client-part1: —
  client-part2: Total leaderboard updates delivered: …
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Shows:
  client-part1: —
  client-part2: === Push latency === (mean, median, p95, p99), computed from the

  received messages' serverTimestamps

  Since the server pushes every 500 ms, each resort's Updates should come out at
  roughly the run time ÷ 0.5 s.

  3. What to run and screenshot

  Part 2 gives the stronger evidence because it adds the total and the push
  latency. From the repository root, after mvn clean package:

  java -jar client-part2/target/ski-client-part2-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar
  http://<EC2_PUBLIC_IP>:8080/ski-servlet 256 | tee results/part2_run.log

  Screenshot:
  - the Target: line, showing the EC2 address
  - the Subscribers connected: 10/10 line
  - the whole === Subscribe Results === block: the per-resort table, Total 
    leaderboard updates delivered, the connection-time line and the === Push 
    latency === block

  This run overwrites results/per_swipe_metrics.csv and
  throughput_over_time.png. If you want to keep the current files, copy them
  somewhere first.

  With Part 1, run:
  java -jar client-part1/target/ski-client-part1-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar
  http://<EC2_PUBLIC_IP>:8080/ski-servlet 256
  Then screenshot Target:, Subscribers connected: 10/10 and the === Subscribe 
  === block. It writes no files.

  4. The gap: counts, not content

  The client never prints a received message, only how many arrived. If the
  grader expects to see the pushed leaderboards themselves, a wscat screenshot
  shows that directly. This command is already documented in server/README.md:

  npx wscat -c ws://<EC2_PUBLIC_IP>:8080/ski-servlet/leaderboard/7

  It shows a new leaderboard JSON with a fresh serverTimestamp about every 500
  ms. A safe set of evidence is that wscat screenshot plus the Part 2 Subscribe
  screenshot.

  If you'd rather show the content from the client instead, the smallest change
  would be in LeaderboardSubscriber: keep the first received message and print
  it once per resort. I haven't made any changes.

      167 +| `=== Warmup Results ===` | 32 workers × 1,000 swipes: attemp
          +ted, successful, failed, retries, runtime, throughput (warmup 
          +successes ÷ warmup runtime) |                                 
      168 +| `=== Main Results ===` | `mainWorkers` threads sharing the r
          +emaining 500,000 swipes: the same metrics, measured over the m
          +ain phase only |                                              
      169 +| `=== Overall Results ===` | Total successful and failed swip
          +es, total runtime (warmup + main), overall throughput |       
      170 +                                                              
      171 +Then each client adds its own sections:                       
      172 +                                                              
      173 +- **Part 1:**                                                 
      174 +  - `=== Connection Statistics (ingest) ===`: `Connection: clo
          +se` responses and connection                                  
      175 +    errors are measured; reconnections and connections opened 
          +are labelled estimates                                        
      176 +  - `=== Subscribe ===`: leaderboard updates received per reso
          +rt, plus WebSocket connection                                 
      177 +    times                                                     
      178 +- **Part 2:**                                                 
      179 +  - ingest latency (mean, median, p95, p99, min, max)         
      180 +  - throughput per resort                                     
      181 +  - `=== Subscribe Results ===`: updates per resort, total lea
          +derboard updates delivered,                                   
      182 +    connection times, drops, reconnects                       
      183 +  - push latency (mean, median, p95, p99)                     
      184 +  - writes the per-swipe CSV and the throughput-over-time char
          +t                                                             
      185 +                                                              
      186 +Each module README defines every metric exactly.              
      187 +                                                              
      188 +## Key design decisions                                       
      189 +                                                              
      190 +- **One generator thread and a bounded queue:** sender threads
          + never generate data, and                                     
      191 +  memory stays bounded at 10,000 queued swipes.               
      192 +- **Synchronous sends over one shared `HttpClient`:** keep-ali
          +ve connections are reused, and                                
      193 +  concurrency equals the number of sender threads.            
      194 +- **Warmup and main phases are timed separately,** so each pha
          +se's throughput uses only its                                 
      195 +  own successes and runtime.                                  
      196 +- **Retries:** up to 5 retries with exponential backoff (100 m
          +s to 1.6 s) on 5xx, 429 and I/O                               
      197 +  errors. A swipe is counted once, whatever its retries.      
      198 +- **Persistent WebSocket subscribers** (one per resort) that r
          +econnect with backoff if a                                    
      199 +  connection drops.                                           
      200 +                                                              
      201 +See [DESIGN.docx](DESIGN.docx) for the full discussion and the
          + Little's Law analysis.                                       
      202 +                                                              
      203  ## Results
      204  
       83 -Test output goes in `results/`. `client-part2` writes `per_swi
          -pe_metrics.csv` and                                           
       84 -`throughput_over_time.png` there on every run.

      208 +| Part 2 output on EC2 (latency, per-resort throughput, push l
          +atency) | [TO BE COMPLETED AFTER EC2 TESTING] |

      213 +The CSV and chart currently in `results/` are not verified EC2
          + results.                                                     
      214 +                                                              
      215 +## Known limitations                                          
      216 +                                                              
      217 +- The server doesn't aggregate swipes, and the leaderboard is 
          +random rather than computed                                   
      218 +  from ingested swipes.                                       
      219 +- Tomcat's default `maxKeepAliveRequests=100` closes each keep
          +-alive connection after 100                                   
      220 +  requests, so clients reconnect regularly under load.        
      221 +- Push latency subtracts a server timestamp from a client time
          +stamp, so it includes clock                                   
      222 +  skew between the two machines.                              
      223 +- WebSocket messages pushed while a subscriber is disconnected
          + are lost, and the client                                     
      224 +  can't detect the loss (there are no sequence numbers).

      158 +### 4. Little's Law baseline

      164 +- It sends one cold request (including TCP setup), then 10 war
          +m requests on the same                                        
      165 +  keep-alive connection.                                      
      166 +- It prints the cold RTT, the average warm RTT, the estimated 
          +connection overhead, and the                                  
      167 +  predicted throughput (threads ÷ average warm RTT) for 16, 32
          +, 64 and 128 threads.

          + reports "Tests run: 0":                                      
      172 +                                                              
      173 +```bash                                                       
      174 +cd client-part2                                               
      175 +java -cp target/classes:target/test-classes client.SwipeGenera
          +torTest   # field ranges over 1,000,000 swipes, path and JSON 
          +format

          +erDemo    # one producer thread, bounded queue, stops at the c
          +onfigured count                                               
      177 +```                                                           
      178 +                                                              
      179 +## How the client works                                       
      180 +                                                              
      181 +- **Generation:** one `SwipeProducer` thread generates every s
          +wipe into an                                                  
      182 +  `ArrayBlockingQueue` with capacity 10,000. The generated val
          +ues are:                                                      
      183 +  - skierID 1–100000, resortID 1–10, liftID 1–40, time 1–360  
      184 +  - seasonID = the current year, dayID = the current day of th
          +e year                                                        
      185 +- **Warmup phase:** 32 `SwipeWorker`s, each sending exactly 1,
          +000 swipes.                                                   
      186 +- **Main phase:** starts after warmup finishes. `mainWorkers` 
          +threads share one                                             
      187 +  `AtomicInteger` holding the remaining 500,000 swipes.       
      188 +- **Sending:** all workers share one `HttpClient` (HTTP/1.1 ke
          +ep-alive) and send                                            
      189 +  synchronously, so concurrency equals the worker count. Each 
          +request has a 10 s timeout.                                   
      190 +- **Retries:** up to 5 retries with backoff of 100, 200, 400, 
          +800 and 1,600 ms. Retries                                     
      191 +  happen on 5xx, 429 and I/O errors; other 4xx responses are n
          +ot retried.                                                   
      192 +  - A swipe counts as successful on any 2xx response, otherwis
          +e as failed after its last                                    
      193 +    attempt.                                                  
      194 +  - Retries are counted separately, as extra attempts.        
      195 +- **Counters:** each worker keeps its own, and they are summed
          + after the workers finish, so                                 
      196 +  no locking is needed.                                       
      197 +- **WebSocket subscribers:** 10 `LeaderboardSubscriber`s, one 
          +per resort.                                                   
      198 +  - They connect before ingest starts and stay open for the wh
          +ole run.                                                      
      199 +  - Each message's receive time and `serverTimestamp` are reco
          +rded.                                                         
      200 +  - A dropped connection is reconnected immediately. Failed at
          +tempts back off from 100 ms                                   
      201 +    up to 5 s.                                                
      202 +                                                              
      203  ## What the clients report
      204  
      205  Both clients print the same phase sections:
      206  
      207  | Section | Contents |
      208  |---|---|
      172 -| `=== Warmup Results ===` | 32 workers × 1,000 swipes: attemp
          -ted, successful, failed, retries, runtime, throughput (warmup 
          -successes ÷ warmup runtime) |                                 
      173 -| `=== Main Results ===` | `mainWorkers` threads sharing the r
          -emaining 500,000 swipes: the same metrics, measured over the m
          -ain phase only |                                              
      174 -| `=== Overall Results ===` | Total successful and failed swip
          -es, total runtime (warmup + main), overall throughput |       
      209 +| `=== Warmup Results ===` | Workers, workload (32,000), attem
          +pted, successful, failed, retries, runtime, throughput (warmup
          + successes ÷ warmup runtime) |                                
      210 +| `=== Main Results ===` | The same metrics for the main phase
          + only (workload 500,000; throughput = main successes ÷ main ru
          +ntime) |                                                      
      211 +| `=== Overall Results ===` | Total successful and failed swip
          +es, total runtime (warmup + main), overall throughput (total s
          +uccesses ÷ total runtime) |                                   
      212  
      176 -Then each client adds its own sections:                       
      213 +Each phase's numbers come from that phase's own workers and ti
          +mer. A `WARNING` line appears                                 
      214 +only if the number of attempted swipes doesn't equal `totalSwi
          +pes`.                                                         
      215  
      178 -- **Part 1:**                                                 
      179 -  - `=== Connection Statistics (ingest) ===`: `Connection: clo
          -se` responses and connection                                  
      180 -    errors are measured; reconnections and connections opened 
          -are labelled estimates                                        
      181 -  - `=== Subscribe ===`: leaderboard updates received per reso

      193 -## Key design decisions                                       
      234 +Part 2 also writes two files:                                 
      235  
      195 -- **One generator thread and a bounded queue:** sender threads
          - never generate data, and                                     
      196 -  memory stays bounded at 10,000 queued swipes.               
      197 -- **Synchronous sends over one shared `HttpClient`:** keep-ali
          -ve connections are reused, and                                
      198 -  concurrency equals the number of sender threads.            
      199 -- **Warmup and main phases are timed separately,** so each pha
          -se's throughput uses only its                                 
      200 -  own successes and runtime.                                  
      201 -- **Retries:** up to 5 retries with exponential backoff (100 m
          -s to 1.6 s) on 5xx, 429 and I/O                               
      202 -  errors. A swipe is counted once, whatever its retries.      
      203 -- **Persistent WebSocket subscribers** (one per resort) that r
          -econnect with backoff if a                                    
      204 -  connection drops.

          +ency,statusCode,resortID`, one row per swipe |                
      239 +| `results/throughput_over_time.png` | Successful swipes per s
          +econd in 10-second buckets |

      244 +- `latency`: milliseconds

       94 +## Client behavior                                            
       95  
      165 -### 5. Client checks (Part 2)                                 
       96 +- **Generation:** one producer thread fills a bounded queue (1
          +0,000), and the sender                                        
       97 +  threads only send.                                          
       98 +- **Phases:**                                                 
       99 +  - warmup: 32 threads × 1,000 swipes                         
      100 +  - main: `mainWorkers` threads sharing the remaining 500,000 
          +swipes                                                        
      101 +- **Sending:** one shared `HttpClient` with keep-alive. Each s
          +ender thread has one request in                               
      102 +  flight at a time.                                           
      103 +- **Retries:** up to 5 retries with exponential backoff on 5xx
          +, 429 and I/O errors. A swipe                                 
      104 +  counts as failed only after its last attempt.               
      105 +- **WebSockets:** 10 subscribers (one per resort) stay connect
          +ed for the whole run and                                      
      106 +  reconnect with backoff if they drop.                        
      107 +- **Output:** both clients print Warmup, Main and Overall resu
          +lts. Each phase's throughput                                  
      108 +  uses only that phase's successes and runtime. Part 1 adds co
          +nnection and subscribe                                        
      109 +  statistics; Part 2 adds latency, per-resort throughput and p
          +ush-latency statistics.                                       
      110  
      167 -These are plain `main()` programs, not JUnit, so `mvn package`
          - reports "Tests run: 0":                                      
      168 -                                                              
      169 -```bash                                                       
      170 -cd client-part2                                               
      171 -java -cp target/classes:target/test-classes client.SwipeGenera
          -torTest   # field ranges over 1,000,000 swipes, path and JSON 
          -format                                                        
      172 -java -cp target/classes:target/test-classes client.SwipeProduc
          -erDemo    # one producer thread, bounded queue, stops at the c
          -onfigured count                                               
      173 -```                                                           
      174 -                                                              
      175 -## How the client works                                       
      176 -                                                              
      177 -- **Generation:** one `SwipeProducer` thread generates every s
          -wipe into an                                                  
      178 -  `ArrayBlockingQueue` with capacity 10,000. The generated val
          -ues are:                                                      
      179 -  - skierID 1–100000, resortID 1–10, liftID 1–40, time 1–360  
      180 -  - seasonID = the current year, dayID = the current day of th
          -e year                                                        
      181 -- **Warmup phase:** 32 `SwipeWorker`s, each sending exactly 1,
          -000 swipes.                                                   
      182 -- **Main phase:** starts after warmup finishes. `mainWorkers` 
          -threads share one                                             
      183 -  `AtomicInteger` holding the remaining 500,000 swipes.       
      184 -- **Sending:** all workers share one `HttpClient` (HTTP/1.1 ke
          -ep-alive) and send                                            
      185 -  synchronously, so concurrency equals the worker count. Each 
          -request has a 10 s timeout.                                   
      186 -- **Retries:** up to 5 retries with backoff of 100, 200, 400, 
          -800 and 1,600 ms. Retries                                     
      187 -  happen on 5xx, 429 and I/O errors; other 4xx responses are n
          -ot retried.                                                   
      188 -  - A swipe counts as successful on any 2xx response, otherwis
          -e as failed after its last                                    
      189 -    attempt.                                                  
      190 -  - Retries are counted separately, as extra attempts.        
      191 -- **Counters:** each worker keeps its own, and they are summed
          - after the workers finish, so

      121  ## Known limitations
      122  
      259 -- The server doesn't aggregate swipes, and the leaderboard is 
          -random rather than computed

          --alive connection after 100                                   
      262 -  requests, so clients reconnect regularly under load.

          -stamp, so it includes clock                                   
      264 -  skew between the two machines.

       ThroughputChart)

     The server and results lines are unchanged.
  4. Retry statement: the old line said the client keeps retrying, which is
     wrong. SwipeWorker tries each swipe 6 times (1 attempt + 5 retries) and
     then counts it as failed. Each attempt can take up to 10 s before timing
     out, plus 3.1 s of backoff across the retries. The line now says the client
     doesn't retry forever, but working through the whole workload this way
     takes a very long time, so stop it with Ctrl+C.
  5. Workload: the code computes the main phase as totalSwipes − 32,000 and
     rejects any value of 32,000 or less.
     - The totalSwipes row now reads: 32,000 warmup + 500,000 main; warmup is
       always 32,000, so main gets totalSwipes − 32,000; the value must be
       greater than 32,000.
     - The main-phase bullet in Client behavior now reads: totalSwipes − 32,000
       swipes (500,000 by default).

  The Markdown checks out: the code fences are balanced, and every Bash block
  contains only real commands (mvn, curl, npx wscat, two java -jar). The
  commands, artifact paths and defaults are unchanged.

  checklist or the Known limitations section; your edits on disk had already
  removed them. I left that as it was. Tell me if you want any of them back.

    running on local port 8080. If you run the jar without a URL argument, every
    connection fails.
  - The test run worked. I deployed your ski-servlet.war to a temporary Tomcat
    copy on port 18080 and ran the existing client-part2 jar with a 40,000-swipe
    workload:
    - Subscribers connected: 10/10
    - each subscriber connected in about 44 ms and received updates (30 in
      total)
    - no drops

    I then stopped that Tomcat and deleted the copy. Your /Library/Tomcat and
    results/ weren't touched, and I changed no files.

  How to fix it
  1. Start the EC2 instance and check its current public IP in the AWS console.
  2. Make sure the security group allows inbound TCP 8080, and that Tomcat on
     EC2 is running with ski-servlet.war deployed.
  3. Check the server: curl http://<IP>:8080/ski-servlet/health should return
     {"status":"UP"}. Then check the WebSocket: npx wscat -c 
     ws://<IP>:8080/ski-servlet/leaderboard/1 should show a message every 500
     ms.
  4. Run the client with the URL as an argument: java -jar 
     client-part2/target/ski-client-part2-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar 
     http://<IP>:8080/ski-servlet. Before you submit, decide whether the default
     URL should be localhost or EC2.

  One thing that makes this hard to spot: LeaderboardSubscriber swallows
  connection errors and just keeps retrying, so all you see is Subscribers 
  connected: 0/10 with no reason. I can add a one-line error log there if you
  want.

✻ Churned for 3m 8s · done 2:16 PM
